  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2, F06.32\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what's been troubling you and what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I came because since my grandson was killed, I just haven’t been right. My daughter, she sits right next to me sometimes, holds my hand—she’s always been a good girl, always worried about me, even when she was little she used to fuss over whether I’d eaten—and I know in my head that I love her. I know that.\n\nBut I can’t feel it the way I should. It’s like there’s no warmth inside me when she’s there, even though I want there to be. That’s what scares me, I think. It feels like something in me went cold.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this feeling of emotional numbness or detachment in other parts of your life, or is it mostly when you're with your daughter?\n\nPatient: Mostly with my daughter, I think… or that’s where I notice it most, because she’s right there, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I raised her, you know, and she’s always been so tender with me—even as a girl she’d bring me a blanket if I looked cold.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, when she sits beside me now, I just… I can’t feel the warmth of loving her inside me. It’s like I know it, but I can’t reach it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this emotional numbness or difficulty feeling warmth or connection with others besides your daughter, like with friends, other family members, or even yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know about friends so much. I haven’t really been paying attention to that. My daughter is the one I notice because she sits right there beside me and holds my hand, and I can look at her and think, that’s my girl, I love her.\n\nShe used to have the softest little curls when she was small—always running around after me, asking if I needed tea, like a tiny nurse. And now she’s grown and still doing that.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I just can’t feel the warmth of loving her inside me. I know it’s there somewhere, or it should be, but I can’t feel it at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed this emotional numbness or difficulty feeling warmth or connection with others besides your daughter, like with friends, other family members, or even yourself?\n\nPatient: I keep coming back to my daughter because that’s where I can tell it’s wrong. She’ll sit right beside me, holding my hand—she has such gentle hands, always did, even when she was a little thing fussing over me—and I know I love her. I know that clear as anything.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed, I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Not the way I should. It’s like I’m looking at love through a window and can’t get to it.\n\nDoctor: Has this emotional numbness affected your connection with other people too, like friends, other family members, or even how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I suppose it must have, but… I keep measuring it by my daughter, because she’s the one right there with me. Friends, other people—I don’t really know, I haven’t been thinking about them much.\n\nIt’s just, I came in because since my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me holding my hand, and I know I love her, I know she’s my child… but I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. That’s the part that frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Besides not feeling the warmth of love for your daughter, have you been feeling down, hopeless, or have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. Down, hopeless—those words feel very big, and I can’t quite tell what’s what anymore.\n\nI don’t think about things I used to enjoy much. There was a time I liked little routines, you know, making tea in a proper cup, sitting by the window, watching the birds fuss at each other. My husband used to say I knew every sparrow by name, which wasn’t true, of course, but I did notice them.\n\nNow when you ask me that, I just… I don’t know if I’ve lost interest or if I’m just not paying attention. Since my grandson was killed, everything feels mixed up, and I keep coming back to that. I’m sorry—I know that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you felt down most days or lost interest in things you used to care about, like watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can separate it out like that. I hear you asking if I feel down most days, or if I don’t care about the little things anymore, and… I just don’t know.\n\nThe tea still gets made sometimes, but then I forget it’s there and it goes cold. My mother used to do that too, leave cups everywhere, and we’d tease her about it—funny what you remember, isn’t it?\n\nBut whether that means I’ve lost interest, I can’t rightly say. Since my grandson died, everything feels… blurred together. I don’t have a neat answer for you.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you’ve felt down or sad most days since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it sad most days. I mean… I know I should be able to say yes or no, but it doesn’t sit that neatly.\n\nSome mornings I’m just standing in the kitchen, and I’ll notice the light on the counter, or think I need to wipe something that’s already clean, and then my mind goes off somewhere else entirely. My sister used to say I could make a whole afternoon out of cleaning one corner of a room.\n\nBut sad… I’m not sure. Since he was killed, it’s like I can’t always tell what feeling is there. I know something is terribly wrong, but I don’t know how to name it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your mood has been persistently low or depressed most days since your grandson passed away?\n\nPatient: Persistently… I’m not sure. That sounds so steady, doesn’t it? Like a blanket laid over every day, and I don’t know if that’s what it is.\n\nSome days I think I’m low, and then I get distracted by something silly, like whether I put the milk back in the refrigerator, or I remember the old grocery shop we used to have near us—the man there always wrapped apples in paper like they were precious.\n\nBut depressed most days… I don’t know how to answer that properly. Since my grandson died, I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I can’t tell you cleanly if it’s a low mood every day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel a steady sadness or low mood, even if it's not intense?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it steady. That word makes it sound like something you could measure, like the old clock in our hallway ticking along. My husband used to wind it every Sunday, and if he forgot, the whole house felt odd without that sound.\n\nSince my grandson died, I know I haven’t been right. But most days, steady sadness… I can’t quite say. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, and sometimes I’m just sort of moving through the room not knowing what I meant to do. I’m sorry, I don’t think I’m answering you very well.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel a general sense of sadness or emptiness, even if it's not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: A general sense… maybe. I’m not sure if emptiness is the right word. Empty makes me think of when the house was first quiet after my husband passed, the way the rooms sounded too big, and I kept leaving the radio on just to have something moving in the air.\n\nWith my grandson… I don’t know. Most days there’s something there, but I can’t always tell if it’s sadness or just me not catching up with myself. I’ll sit down with a cup of tea and then remember it’s gone cold, and I don’t remember drinking any of it. But whether that’s emptiness, I… I’m not certain.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to it. Not just the birds or tea, though I used to like little things like that. The robins used to come right up near the back step, bold as anything, and my husband would say they were waiting for rent.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed… it’s like something in me won’t warm up. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know that as plainly as I know my own name. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s a terrible thing to say about your own child, isn’t it? I don’t mean I don’t love her. I do. I just… can’t seem to feel it properly.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things you used to, like watching the birds or other little routines you mentioned before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. It isn’t only the little routines, though those feel flat now too. I came in because of something worse, really.\n\nSince my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her socks under the sofa cushions for some reason, drove me mad looking for them before school.\n\nBut now, when she’s there with me, I can’t feel the warmth of loving her inside me. Not at all. It’s like I know it in my head, but nothing rises up in my chest the way it should. That frightens me more than the birds, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things you used to, like watching the birds or other little routines you mentioned before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know I’ve said this, but it’s not really the birds I came about. I mean, I used to notice them, and the kettle, and all those small things, but since my grandson was killed it’s… it’s more frightening than just not enjoying a cup of tea.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling won’t come through, even when she’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you found that you don’t take pleasure in most activities that you used to enjoy, not just watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep circling back to the same thing, don’t I? I know that. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s just… absent. That’s what frightens me.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you found that you don’t take pleasure in most activities that you used to enjoy, even things beyond watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, but it’s hard to talk about it as “activities,” if you see what I mean. I used to have my little ways, and now they all seem sort of beside the point.\n\nBut what brought me here—what keeps frightening me—is my daughter. Since my grandson was killed, she can sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know it plain as anything. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Not the way a mother should. It’s just… not there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days since your grandson passed away, you’ve felt a general sense of sadness or emptiness, even if it wasn’t intense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it that neatly, doctor. “Most days” makes it sound like I’ve been keeping count, and I haven’t. Since my grandson died, I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I can’t tell you cleanly if it’s a low mood every day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel a steady sadness or low mood, even if it's not intense?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it steady. That word makes it sound like something you could measure, like the old clock in our hallway ticking along. My husband used to wind it every Sunday, and if he forgot, the whole house felt odd without that sound.\n\nSince my grandson died, I know I haven’t been right. But most days, steady sadness… I can’t quite say. Sometimes there’s a heaviness, and sometimes I’m just sort of moving through the room not knowing what I meant to do. I’m sorry, I don’t think I’m answering you very well.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel a general sense of sadness or emptiness, even if it's not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: A general sense… maybe. I’m not sure if emptiness is the right word. Empty makes me think of when the house was first quiet after my husband passed, the way the rooms sounded too big, and I kept leaving the radio on just to have something moving in the air.\n\nWith my grandson… I don’t know. Most days there’s something there, but I can’t always tell if it’s sadness or just me not catching up with myself. I’ll sit down with a cup of tea and then remember it’s gone cold, and I don’t remember drinking any of it. But whether that’s emptiness, I… I’m not certain.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you found that you no longer take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to it. Not just the birds or tea, though I used to like little things like that. The robins used to come right up near the back step, bold as anything, and my husband would say they were waiting for rent.\n\nBut since my grandson was killed… it’s like something in me won’t warm up. My daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know that as plainly as I know my own name. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me. It’s a terrible thing to say about your own child, isn’t it? I don’t mean I don’t love her. I do. I just… can’t seem to feel it properly.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things you used to, like watching the birds or other little routines you mentioned before?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose I have. It isn’t only the little routines, though those feel flat now too. I came in because of something worse, really.\n\nSince my grandson was killed, my daughter can sit right beside me and hold my hand, and I know—of course I know—I love her. She’s my girl. I remember when she was little, she used to tuck her socks under the sofa cushions for some reason, drove me mad looking for them before school.\n\nBut now, when she’s there with me, I can’t feel the warmth of loving her inside me. Not at all. It’s like I know it in my head, but nothing rises up in my chest the way it should. That frightens me more than the birds, I think.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things you used to, like watching the birds or other little routines you mentioned before?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know I’ve said this, but it’s not really the birds I came about. I mean, I used to notice them, and the kettle, and all those small things, but since my grandson was killed it’s… it’s more frightening than just not enjoying a cup of tea.\n\nMy daughter can sit right beside me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know she’s my child. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. It’s like the feeling won’t come through, even when she’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Since your grandson passed away, have you found that you don’t take pleasure in most activities that you used to enjoy, not just watching the birds or making tea?\n\nPatient: Yes… though I keep circling back to the same thing, don’t I? Since my grandson was killed, she can sit right next to me, holding my hand, and I know I love her. I know it plain as anything. But I can’t feel the warmth of it inside me at all. Not the way a mother should. It’s just… not there.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days since your grandson passed away, you’ve felt a general sense of sadness or emptiness, even if it wasn’t intense?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say it that neatly, doctor. “Most days” makes it sound like I’ve been keeping count, and I haven’t. I’m not sure if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days since your grandson passed away, you’ve felt a general sense of sadness or emptiness, even if it’s not overwhelming?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can put it that way. I don’t know, doctor. “Depressed mood” sounds like something you could pin down neatly, and I can’t seem to do that.

There are times I feel very low, yes, but then I’ll find myself thinking about whether I left the back door unlocked, or how my neighbor’s roses used to lean over the fence in June—she never trimmed them properly, but they were beautiful all the same.
